ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e9120	Mr President. Mrs. Colleague! Honourable Members! Dear initiators and initiators of popular desire! Dear listeners! A popular desire for education must always be most welcome to one of the politicians responsible for education, this too was welcome. It has covered a number of points that directly concern the universities, and I will turn to them more precisely. It has also formulated some general points that are important. The first point is extremely important because it highlights on the one hand the autonomy of educational institutions and, above all, focuses on the objectives of education. I believe that this is a key path that we, as politicians, are responsible for the objectives and the achievement of these objectives, but that we are opening the way to those who know best how to achieve these goals, and these are the educational institutions themselves. And I can already exemplify this at the first point that the universities are involved in, that is the point pedagogical education-new. There too, I believe, it makes sense for politics to say which educators we want, but it does not regulate in detail how these educators are to be produced, so to speak, but simply sets the objectives and leaves the institutions to pursue and achieve these objectives as best they can. It is also clear – and this also explains popular desire very clearly and very, very, very well, and I am in line with my Mrs. Schmied – that those institutions that are now working on teacher education should continue to do it, do it more and more cooperatively, in close cooperation. The popular desire is very, very striking, the responsibility of the universities for educational education. It is obvious that good teacher training needs to be knowledge-based that it must be borne by science, both in general education and development psychology as well as in vocational training, in specialist training. Any other education, which is not based on science, is second or third-hand training and is therefore necessary and inevitably poorer. Therefore, the integration of universities into the educational education system is new indispensable and a central moment of qualitative training, which in turn is a central element and moment of every good education in Austria. For good education, we need the best teachers we can only get, and these best teachers need to be trained scientifically. The second of the four points that concern universities in particular is the financing of high schools. It is very striking that it needs to be sufficient for it to be increased. They all know that we are in very, very difficult times and that it has nevertheless succeeded in providing more funding for universities. This is, I believe, a clear commitment by this government to this important topic of the future, and it is at least a partial fulfillment of what popular desire demands – but it is not enough. Clearly, universities, universities can be financed infinitely, they will always have certain limits of their capacities, which are inevitable. With so much money, they will have these limits, which show international examples. That is why there is also a need to finance a study place, and there is a need for access schemes, where I stress that these access schemes should not stop anyone from studying, that students should only be guided in their decisions that they do not go into the subjects that are already overcrowded, but go into subjects where there is still room, into subjects where there is more future prospects, into subjects that we need in economic terms, but which also offer more career opportunities for graduates. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] This is a steering of student flows and not a limitation of student flows – this is very important to me! But it is also important to stress that it is clear to me that, whether it is economically difficult times, like now or even better times, which may and hopefully come back again, we must necessarily increase the private share of higher education funding. As far as the public share of higher education funding is concerned, we are far ahead of the world. Austria takes a lot of public money into its hands for higher education funding, but Austria has extremely little private money. We have half of what is the average in the EU, we have a fifth of what is the average of private funding for universities in the OECD, and that is why, I think, it is fair, meaningful and effective if we have to be socially compatible with study contributions that no one is allowed to stop from studying, so that the private share of higher education funding in Austria also increases. That's essential. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Only with a mix of public and private money will we achieve the goal of 2 percent of gross domestic product for this area by 2020, otherwise we will never and never achieve that. Another point in popular desire concerning universities and universities is lifelong learning. You can only support that. I know we still have a lot to do, but I can say that last year – and you know this – a strategy for lifelong learning was decided by the Federal Government, with a wealth of measures, with ten lines of action. There, the universities and universities will be actively involved in the realization of this concern of popular desire. One final goal that popular desire formulates is the academic rate, the better social mix of students. There are many demands, too. However, one must also relativize, also in the sense of Member Dr. Grünewald: Not every successful life must go through a university, also the economy, the economy – the example of Switzerland shows this wonderfully – needs a wealth of qualifications. We must also not make the mistake of transferring the ancient dualism, which is also a hierarchy, namely mind/body – mind is good, body is bad, which is ultimately a platonic heritage – unsolicited and unreflected to our choice of profession and professional hierarchy. We must appreciate all professions and all the ways, not just intellectuals and not just universities, and that is why a rigid schilling on an OECD-defined academic rate is not a target for us, if we count on everything that matters in other countries, quite passive. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] I can only say to the social mix: Yes, it could be better in Austria, but I can also say that, for example, study contributions or access schemes – this shows a wealth of international studies – do not have a negative or a positive impact on social interference. To achieve a better social mix, you have to start much earlier. In the end, this is not a task of universities, but is primarily a pre-educational task. That is why it is also good for the educational population to consider education as a whole. Free access to higher education begins in kindergarten and preschool education. Finally, let me say something in summary and also take up an appeal which my Mrs Schmied has already expressed and which I fully agree with, namely, the appeal to shape a culture of success. Especially when Mr. Hengstschläger has been quoted several times today and his appeal has been quoted to see more strengths than the weaknesses: In the education sector, we tend to constantly focus on the weaknesses of the Austrian education system and not to see its strengths. And the Austrian education system still has considerable strengths, otherwise we would not be where we are. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] [[Abg. That's right!]] We have excellent schools, including the much-fed, often put down as a dead end, which are excellent schools in the federal states, which contribute much to our having a well-trained population. We have excellent universities that are wrongfully mispronounced. We should also, especially in the education sector and in all legitimate concerns to improve it – Schola etiam est reformanda, that is clear, the school must also be renewed and reformed again and again – taking into account that it is much better than it is often put up. Let us also consider the strengths, see the strengths and appreciate them!

Today we are undoubtedly living in a laizist society, and we understand laizity as autonomy of the civil and political sphere towards the religious and ecclesial, but not towards the moral sphere. This is a value that belongs to the achievements of our civilization. Many people today call themselves atheists. But even if they call themselves atheists, they know very well that their roots, their cultural, civilized origin, are their identity in Christianity. Nevertheless, a state has occurred in our society in which religion is to be openly suppressed from public space and only to be communicated in private. That means religion should be hidden. In this context, three fundamental questions have to be asked. The famous question: What can the worldlyly neutral state actually want in terms of values? – The so-called Böckenförde-Dilemma. Böckenförde, a German state lawyer, said: Freedom-written, worldlyly neutral state cannot create or guarantee the values of which it lives – it therefore needs, for its sake, the great donors of values: above all, the religions. If he himself involves himself in values with all his administrative apparatus and ultimately presents values, he ultimately puts his identity at risk. By State These large values donors in as many places as possible in the public space, he secures doubles: (a) protect itself from cross-border crossings which may threaten its identity; (b) the introduction of values with that seriousness, as they claim only religions. The second question of principle: What ethics is actually taught in ethics? And the third question of principle: Should, may, will the State become a monopolistic teacher on ethics issues? – This is currently being violently rejected by our German neighbours. There are some who have experienced this in the former GDR and with this wealth of experience in the background reject this vehement. Thus, in ethics education, a variety of values of a pluralist society is now to be represented, but in the final effect it turns out: Everything is good, everything is equally valid, ultimately indifferent. If all religious and antireligious views are equally valid in an ethics class, they will soon be indifferent to the disciple seeking meaning and hold. One is forcing the liberation from the past, because it is still progressive. On the other hand, religious teaching stands with its reliability in terms of values and centuries old tradition. And another point: He works practically with an open visor. You know what's being taught there. When teaching ethics, it is very nebulous, we do not know that very well to this day. You can't live morals if you don't know a scale. The constant values have raised people, not the popularity, and that is, freedom needs scale, freedom needs orientation, freedom needs hold. Otherwise everything is allowed, everything possible. [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] Finally, I would like to present a quote from the very well-known philosopher Martin Rhonheimer, also with regard to the state-bearing and, above all, democratically important question of religious teaching: “In view of the challenges of multiculturality, in particular the presence of an increasing number of Muslim fellow citizens in European countries, who are not on the ground of the common Western and Christian heritage, Europe will have to become increasingly aware of its Christian roots – not in order to ‘rechristianize’ public life in the sense of turning away from the secularization process and to discriminate against non-Christians accordingly, but precisely in the opposite sense: to preserve and, if necessary, defend the peace-making, integrating power of a secular political culture based on the recognition of human rights and fundamental political freedoms. Perhaps in the future, it will become increasingly clear that we should remember the ultimately Christian roots of modern secularity and political culture precisely to defend and develop this secularity in a successful way. This will also enable us to offer to those whose cultural origin is different a real chance of integration: not by forcing them to become Christians themselves, but without denying that our secular world is a mature fruit of the historical civilizational power of Christianity.” [[President Dr. Graf takes over the chairmanship.]] Ladies and gentlemen, it is so that we do not want to abolish religious teaching, we want to maintain religious teaching. To abolish religious teaching means simply and simply to remove our democracy, our state and, above all, our freedom from the ground under our feet. In this sense a clear confession without If and But to religious teaching.

The aim is that we can help 270 people to complete compulsory schooling and further 330 people improve their basic skills. 600 people are thus given new opportunities in Tyrol alone. However, it should also be stressed that for the first time uniform quality criteria are laid down for these courses in all federal states. These standards concern, for example, the content of courses, the structure of courses, the qualifications of the lecturers, but also the educational institutions themselves are strictly examined in relation to the necessary infrastructure. Thus, as with the new upper level to be decided on today, an Austrian system is being created to ensure that compulsory school qualifications achieve a uniform level. We help with this article-15a agreement of a group of people, which is mostly low-income anyway. And if you are looking at the cost of an average of €6,600 for a master's degree, I believe that we are taking a sensible measure and congratulate the Minister of Education on this milestone that is set in education policy. ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e13778	Mr Waldner spoke on a point of view. The speaking time should not exceed 10 minutes. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e13800	Madam President. High house! After Mrs Korun mentioned that I have already discussed this with her in detail in the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Human Rights, I am pleased that I have the opportunity to comment on this subject in the short term. She pointed out that it was said, and I say it again: Due to the historical and geopolitical situation, Austria has a long tradition in intercultural and interreligious dialogue, especially with the Muslim world and especially with Islam in Europe. The legal recognition of Islam in Austria in 1912 has contributed a great deal to the fact that for decades these dialogue activities also have a very prominent place on our foreign policy agenda. As one of three objectives, the newly adopted Austrian foreign culture concept 2011 includes the “performance of a sustainable contribution to global confidence building and peacekeeping through initiatives in the field of culture and religion dialogue”. Austria therefore intends to continue to set up more initiatives on issues of intercultural and interreligious dialogue in the future. This will contribute to the reduction of stereotypes and thereby also to peace and security. The dialogue of cultures and religions should contribute to strengthening democracy and universal respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms, including freedom of religion and conscience. The dialogue is an important instrument for promoting intercultural and interreligious living together and integration in Austria and Europe. For years, projects with intergovernmental organisations, tertiary educational institutions and civil society have been organised and carried out through a task force of “dialogue of cultures” embedded in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. These activities use the dialogue as an instrument to identify and safeguard universal values such as democracy, human rights and religious freedom. And precisely in this tradition, ladies and gentlemen, Austria signed this founding agreement for the Centre for Interreligious and Intercultural Dialogue by Mr Vice-Chanzler in October 2011. The Centre was founded as an international organization based in Vienna. The Treaty establishing the European Union lays down international rules for the structure and orientation of this centre and provides a clear commitment to the Universal Declaration of Human Rights of the United Nations and thereby also to freedom of religion and conscience. [[Calls to the Greens.]] For the first time, this centre creates a stable international platform for dialogue between world religions, and that in the middle of Vienna. The need for a systematic interreligious and intercultural dialogue in our world today is probably undisputed. I can confirm this myself because of my personal perception – I am only eight months in my office. I have already had many discussions with religious representatives, with politicians dealing with these issues, at home and abroad, most recently in Kosovo, in Priština, with religious representatives, in Jakarta five weeks ago, in Doha about a month ago and this week still in Maskat, where I will again talk with representatives of Islam and with politicians who are also pursuing this very subject. We therefore consider this centre to be an important initiative for international dialogue, and we have deliberately decided to be co-founders with Spain and also Saudi Arabia. Several other European cities have applied for the seat of this centre: Madrid, Geneva, London. – Would it have been better for you if this center had been located there? [[Abg. May. Korun: Yes! Yes!]] We are proud that it is in Vienna. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] And that it is located in Vienna is again recognition and confirmation of our long-standing tradition and credibility in this area. The Centre is an enrichment for Vienna, an enrichment for Vienna as a headquarters for international organisations and seamlessly integrates into our previous activities for dialogue and for peace. The accession to this Treaty is, of course, open to other members, and there are several countries that are already interested in joining this Treaty. The dialogue centre is not limited to the monotheistic religions of Judaism, Christianity and Islam, but explicitly also refers to other beliefs: Buddhism, Hinduism. These beliefs will be represented in the Executive Board from the beginning. The Holy See has indicated that it is seeking observer status for this centre and that it wants to play an active role here. The Apostolic Nuncio made a very positive statement to this centre at the reception of the President of the Federal Republic of Germany a few days ago, where I was present, before the diplomatic corps gathered. And the President of the Islamic Faith in Austria has repeatedly expressly supported the initiative in public statements. None of the religions that are represented here will dominate or be able to implement or enforce their view. That is why we have chosen this form of international organisation: a binding agreement between states with clear structures and decision-making rules. The Centre is based on clear principles, and the founding agreement clearly and clearly refers to the Universal Declaration of Human Rights of the United Nations, in particular freedom of belief and conscience. We will, of course, implement our dialogue priorities, which are known to you: Religious pluralism, freedom of religion and conscience, protection of religious minorities and strengthening the role and leadership functions of women. [[Abg. May. Korun: In Saudi Arabia, too?]] We see in the centre an opportunity to make a contribution to the enforcement of precisely these values and these principles, especially in those countries where they are so far less or less respected. The success of this centre depends on the religious communities and churches involved, which are the promoters of dialogue. In the coming weeks, only for information, an Austrian association will be established, which will prepare the establishment of the Centre. Members will be the three Parties who have signed the founding Convention: Austria, Spain and Saudi Arabia, and all decisions will be taken in agreement with these three states and those founding members. We are currently preparing the Government paper on the ratification of the founding agreement and we hope that the parliamentary approval procedure can be initiated in a few weeks. I am confident that this centre will make a constructive contribution, and I hope that the initiative will also receive your support in the framework of parliamentary treatment. Finally, ladies and gentlemen, allow me to express a personal wish which I have already expressed on the margins of the Committee on Human Rights: Give this dialogue, this initiative an opportunity, and evaluate this initiative on the basis of the concrete results! - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e17441	Mr Waldner has now come to speak. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e17462	Mr President. High house! I would also like to confirm here in plenary that this concern is taken very seriously in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and is regularly addressed by us. I myself, as announced in the Committee, have repeatedly addressed the question of the recognition of the German-speaking minority in my visits, in Laibach, to my official Slovenian interlocutors. The President of the Federal Republic of Germany has raised the issue in Liège to the President of the Republic of Turkey. Let me briefly outline the starting situation: This group of Old-Austrian German-speaking people in Slovenia comprises approximately 1,600 people, about 1 permill of the population, spread across Slovenia with a concentration in six cities. The group is explicitly recognised in the 2002 bilateral cultural agreement with Slovenia. In any case, from an Austrian point of view, the cultural agreement is an important step towards anchoring this group as an autonomous minority in the Slovenian constitution. And the Slovenian Constitution guarantees these minorities the free commitment of belonging to a people's group, including the care of their own culture and the use of their own writing and language. A recognition as an autonomous group with certain special rights is currently not intended for the German-speaking minority, but only for the Italian and Hungarian peoples. President Türk, this was also discussed several times in the Committee, proposed to our President a membership of the German-speaking People's Group in the so-called Cooperation Council. This is to be set up for new ethnic groups in the former Yugoslavia. Such participation could be a useful intermediate step towards full recognition. I would like to assure you once again that we will continue to do so in the international forums, in the Human Rights Council, of course in the Council of Europe, and that the objective remains the anchoring of the German-speaking peoples' group as an autonomous group in the Constitution. In the future, we will continue to support the associations of the German-speaking People's Group financially – through our embassy in Liège and through the Ministry of Foreign Affairs directly. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e17823	I'll call the 10. Agenda item. The debate opens up to Mr Vock. Please, my colleague.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e17848	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! Human rights are known to be indivisible. Although interference in the internal affairs of another State as in this case is always questionable, a violation of human rights is generally condemnable. That is why we would have liked or supported Mr Korun's request, but unfortunately he did not find a majority in committee, which is why there is a negative committee report here. This request only says that it is necessary to ensure that only internationally recognised criminal offences are prosecuted, that it is not arbitrary detention. The Court of First Instance has held that the Court of First Instance should only have jurisdiction before ordinary independent courts, that there should be no death penalty on persons under 18 or without proper court proceedings. Torture should be prevented from being released from political prisoners who are detained for the peaceful exercise of their right to freedom of expression and freedom of assembly, and in general the abolition of the death penalty in that state should be rethinked. In its amendment, the government parties focused only on the fight against terrorism and not on the fundamental violation of human rights in this case. That is why we also reject the government proposal.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e17884	Mr Waldner has now come to speak. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e17905	Mr President. High house! I would like to oppose briefly the accusation expressed in today's short debate to the Dialogue Centre that the abolition of the death penalty, specifically in Saudi Arabia, by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs is not carried out with the necessary emphasis. The abolition of the death penalty worldwide is one of the priorities of Austrian human rights policy. This was also confirmed by the National Council's resolution on the death penalty in the summer of 2011. Austria, together with its partners, is committed to the abolition of the death penalty in general and intensively to non-compliance in the individual case. In concrete terms, interventions based on the EU guidelines should be mentioned, in particular in the case of cruel forms of execution, in the case of juvenile offenders, in the case of unfair procedures, in the case of pregnant women or people with mental disabilities. In this sense, Mrs Korun, we are constantly calling for the abolition of the death penalty or, as a first step, at least a suspension of the death penalty in bilateral contacts with the Saudi interlocutors in Saudi Arabia. This is only in advance and in addition to the previous debate, because it is also related to the current issue, with the discussion on the draft new anti-terrorism law in Saudi Arabia, which we are also following with great attention. It should be said that measures against terrorism are in the interests of the entire state community. However, we are convinced that such measures must take place in compliance with human rights and rule of law. The draft law in Saudi Arabia itself is by no means undisputed in this country, and it is not yet clear whether and in what form the draft will continue to be handled in Saudi Arabia. An early adoption of this law is, in any case, not foreseeable according to the state of knowledge we have in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. Of course, Austria will not hesitate to express its concerns if the final draft is present and there are again provisions that are illegal to human rights. Our aim is to improve human rights protection in actions against terrorism worldwide, not only in Saudi Arabia. Therefore, within the framework of the UN, especially during the Austrian membership of the Security Council, we have on every occasion raised our voice for strengthening the protection of human rights in the global fight against terrorism. As a member of the UN Human Rights Council, but also at regional and bilateral level, we are going to be very close. The Commission will continue its work on this enhanced protection of human rights and on the rule of law. Thank you.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e17948	Next speaker: Mr Sacher. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e17969	Mr President. High house! Dear ladies and gentlemen! It is known that the Republic of Austria has a seat in the UN Human Rights Council. Not least because of this, it is already clear that Austria is not only a country in which human rights are held with the utmost degree of self-evidentness, but also that we are always committed to human rights internationally, where they are disregarded. In fact, it would no longer be necessary in Parliament to do so. However, if this proposal we have tabled here today is debated and hopefully decided on in the Committee, this is taking place in the light of the fact that, in the light of acts of terrorism and their fight against them, on the one hand, and the protection of human rights on the other hand, which are threatened by anti-terrorism measures, there is a tension that policy must deal with. Terrorist acts are serious crimes, threaten society and call for innocent lives. Anti-terrorism strategies are therefore becoming increasingly necessary at international, national and regional level. The international community requires the fight against cross-border crime. Steps have been taken for international legal assistance and extradition agreements and will continue. Police cooperation is a command of the hour. That's the one side. Terrorist action is, however, very often linked to significant interventions in fundamental and freedom rights and human rights. That's the other side. There is a danger, and it is often a reality that some governments are taking the lead in dealing with the rule of law and reducing human rights. Finding the right way to do this is one of the major challenges facing politics. As already stated in the Committee, however, we believe that this should not only be projected on a country, as Mrs Korun did with her special request, but that we should also refer to all countries where the risk of human rights being eroded and disregarded under the pretext of combating terrorism. That is why we have tabled this request together with the ÖVP, and I request your approval.

The current item on the agenda now deals with the bloody reduction of conflict minerals in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. This country, deeply drawn by its former Belgian colonial regime, later dictators and subsequent civil wars – “African World War” – lies in the heart of Africa. With about 70 million inhabitants – the fourth largest state in Africa – around 200 groups and the second largest area in all African countries – 2.34 million square kilometres – the state is one of the poorest countries in the world despite its natural richness. Due to the decades of exploitation, Congo takes its last place in the UN Human Development Index of 187. Ladies and gentlemen! We want to allow people in the Republic of Congo long overdue aid for self-help by means of the resolution which is being adopted today. Therefore, we urge the Federal Government, and in particular the Federal Minister for European and International Affairs, to work within the EU to ensure that conflict minerals are consistently taken into account in the EU's dialogue with the Democratic Republic of the Congo in order to encourage them to increase the protection of the affected local population through greater transparency in terms of the reduction and trade of conflict minerals and, secondly, to reform and professionalise the security sector of the Democratic Republic of Congo within the EU. Today's joint decision sets out a clear sign of our solidarity with the men, women and children living in this crisis-stricken state. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e18404	Mr Waldner has now come to speak. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e18426	Mr President. High house! I should like to say from the point of view of the foreign policy request of Mrs Korun: The illegal...
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e18483	Please, Mr. State Secretary.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e18499	Mr President. With regard to Mrs Korun's request, I should like to say, from the point of view of foreign policy, that: The illegal degradation of raw materials by various rebel groups in the Democratic Republic of the Congo is a serious problem from a human and security point of view. Such activities are a danger to peace and stability in the country. We therefore attach great importance to peaceful and economically sustainable development in Congo. Sustainability in this context means that people in particular can benefit from the richness of raw materials in the region. The Federal Government therefore shares the demand for greater transparency in terms of the reduction of raw materials in the Congo, but also pursues this objective in other conflict regions. Together with our partners in the EU, we use international forums to address the problem of so-called conflict minerals and to develop solutions. At United Nations level, there has been cooperation with the Republic of Congo in the framework of the so-called Kimberley process, which Austria fully supports since 2003. In this certification procedure, participating States must comply with precise guidelines in order to be able to sell raw diamonds as conflict-free. Although there are still problems here, we see the strong commitment of the Republic of Congo in this process as an important contribution to the fight against illegal trade in raw materials. We will therefore continue to play an active part in this initiative. Another way to limit illegal trade in so-called conflict minerals is to increase the transparency of money flows in connection with the reduction and trade in conflict zones. The European Commission is working on a number of measures in this respect. In addition, the EU is also making a major contribution to professionalising and reforming the security sector in Congo. All these measures are essential in order to combat the illegal reduction of raw materials and to improve the safety situation for the population in a sustainable way. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e18945	Mr President. High house! The consistent commitment to human rights is a long-term priority of Austrian foreign policy. We use all available instruments, bilateral contacts, but also multilateral cooperation with the partners in the EU, the Council of Europe, the OSCE, the United Nations. The UN Human Rights Council is the central forum for this organisation for the protection and promotion of human rights. Austria was known to have been elected to this body for three years last year, and this membership offers us the opportunity to further expand our traditionally strong and internationally recognised commitment to human rights with concrete initiatives. We stand for a strong Human Rights Council that effectively addresses specific country situations. With the recent special meeting on the human rights situation in Syria on 2 February. The Council of Human Rights has responded quickly and effectively to the serious human rights violations in that country, with the establishment of an independent committee of inquiry and then a special rapporteur on Syria. For the coming period, we have focused on several issues: the protection of freedom of religion and conscience, the protection of religious minorities, the promotion of freedom of the media and the protection of journalists and the promotion of children's rights and their protection from violence and exploitation. On these three aforementioned priorities, Austria will set out concrete initiatives in the Human Rights Council in the coming years. We will also contribute to these priorities in other relevant forums, in the EU, the UN Alliance of Civilizations, the OSCE, the Council of Europe and UNESCO. We will also increase the use of membership of the Human Rights Council in order to continue the previous priorities in this body. These include minorities, internally displaced persons, women's rights and the strengthening of the rule of law. A brief comment on the specific request concerning the protection of journalists: As the start of our initiative for the protection of journalists, an expert round of consultations took place in Vienna last November, where state representatives, representatives of international organisations and civil society took part. I led a panel myself in this event. The results of these consultations will be incorporated into a resolution that will bring Austria into the Human Rights Council this year. We want to place particular emphasis on the problem of impunity of attacks on journalists and on the prevention of attacks and to focus on them. Unfortunately, the killing of a French journalist recently in Syria has again demonstrated the sad topicality of this Austrian initiative. Our aim is to anchor the protection of journalists firmly on the international human rights agenda. As regards the request concerning recommendations from the UN Human Rights Council, I would like to say the following: The universal examination of the human rights situation for all states takes place regularly within the framework of the so-called Universal Periodic Review, the Universal State Review. We first submitted to this examination last year, and we also take the implementation of the recommendations in exchange with civil society very seriously. A separate UPR Steering Group was set up, consisting of the representatives of the BKA Constitutional Service, representatives of our department and representatives of civil society who accompany the implementation process of the UPR. Some of these UPR recommendations to Austria also concern the ratification of the Optional Protocol to the Anti-Folter Convention, which is subject to the OPCAT Enforcement Act of 7. ==References====External links==* Official website Finally, a comment on the request concerning the fight against torture: Austria, together with the EU partners, is promoting an absolute global ban on torture, and we consistently benefit from existing EU instruments. In order to ensure a coherent approach, the EU guidelines on combating torture are currently being reviewed and integrated into the new EU country strategies. – Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e19220	Mr President. Mr. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! On the theme of strengthening human rights: As mentioned earlier, Austria was subject to an examination of human rights compliance in Geneva in January 2011, and 161 recommendations were made to improve the situation in Austria. A lot of it has been implemented. A very positive turn also has the decision to introduce the OPCAT Enforcement Act, valid from 1. ==References== It falls within the jurisdiction of the People's Procuratorate, and in the meantime 15 necessary planning posts have already been decided for this purpose. OPCAT is about strict respect for human rights in places of liberty. This means not only judicial institutions and police inspections, but also first places for asylum seekers, barracks, psychiatric institutions and programmes for disabled people. With regard to human rights: If you start a data query on the Internet on “Infringements against Human Rights” you would probably be able to deal with it for weeks. ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e19268	Next speaker: Mrs Gessl-Ranftl. 2 minutes speaking time. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e19289	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! High house! According to Amnesty International, torture belongs to everyday life in around 80 countries on earth. What do you mean by torture? – Torture is understood as the targeted addition of mental or physical suffering – from people to people. Tortures have the purpose of breaking the will and resistance of the tortured. They are used for punishment, extortion of confessions, humiliation or intimidation. Torture and abuse hurt, spread fear and terror. They destroy the personality and dignity of the tortured. Many victims are silent because of fear, helplessness, but also shame. To return to normal life after torture and abuse is extremely difficult and, in most cases, certainly not feasible. “Theft and other forms of cruel, degrading and inhuman treatment and punishment can never be justified,” says UN Secretary General Ban Ki-moon. There are absolutely no exceptions! Neither war nor internal instability nor any other emergency justify torture and abuse. The absolute prohibition of torture and abuse is enshrined in the United Nations Convention on Human Rights. It is our duty to try to ensure that this ban is actually complied with and, in particular, in third countries. Victims must be actively supported. We have the duty and have to act on other countries that: ratify and create prevention mechanisms for the UN anti-torture convention and the Additional Protocol. I would therefore ask you, ladies and gentlemen, to accept this motion for a resolution. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e20243	We arrive at the 16th. Agenda item. We are going into the debate. First of all, the applicant, Mr Oellinger. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e20270	My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! I know, at first readings at the end of an agenda, that nobody wants to stop; I also make it relatively short. In essence, in contrast to the previous regulation – in which replacement care is possible, but only within the framework of the funds provided for in a corresponding support fund – we insist that a legal claim be created for caregivers. We insist that only the legal claim for the caregivers – and this is primarily the case, largely family members who care for a long time – is possible in the event of illness, vacation or other grounds of prevention. They need this urgently, because the current situation, in which family members are very burdened, we know this from the experience and from the judge – often leading to an exaggeration of these family members or has already led to it. We want to support and strengthen this possibility for relatives to care for their family members. This requires a measure such as the creation of this legal right, beyond the debate, whether we can afford it financially. This is an urgent need in this area, with which we can not only ensure for the future that relatives can take care of, but that we can also reduce much higher costs in other areas – through professional care.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-01-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00141_d7e20656	Mr President. High house! On my request or on the amendment of the Civil Service Law, in brief: We find or I find, the § 109 Civil Service Law is no longer entirely modern. It also contradicts Austrian case law. In this context, a brief discussion will be made on the following: In the event of minor, minimum breach of service by staff members, the Head of Service or Head of Staff may issue a written warning. This written reminder must then be taken over in writing by the official. That is, the official, the official must confirm it in writing. However, the staff member has no appeal against it, which is not provided for. Thus, a single person, just the head of the service, is open to the door and gate when it comes to exerting pressure on a certain circle of officials. He may issue written reminders which may harm the official for three years. If he volunteers to change his job in his office, this is a disadvantage for three years. Then the written reminder must be removed from the staff record. This cannot happen, however, because the personal records are numbered, so that can no longer be removed. Thus, this so-called written admonition is liable to the official, to the official for a lifetime or for his entire service. We have nothing against the written admonition. This is supposed to be the case, in the case of minor, minor service violations. But one cannot be in a rule of law such as Austria, namely that the official who has no appeal against this subjective individual decision of a Head of Unit. It's no longer contemporary. There is neither jurisdiction nor administrative punishment. There are recessions everywhere, you can complain everywhere, you can call everywhere. Only here are the official, the servant's hands tied, he can't do anything about it. He has to confirm it, as I said, even with his signature, with date and signature, and it is to his detriment, as has been said, throughout his life or all his service. This is not all right from our point of view and is of course changed as soon as possible. - Thank you.

And in common it has been said: all citizens in a community. That's how it was, and that's how it can be seen. ==References====External links== This has been clarified in the century, because at that time of course this struggle for forest and pasture had already progressed massively, and some of those who are already in power in Tyrol at the time wanted to acquire these grains so to speak. And I believe that this situation in conflict shows the importance of respect for the Constitution and the importance of compliance with the Constitutional Court. That is why I am convinced that we in the Committee will not be convinced that there must be a change of law here. The rights of the communities are particularly important. Today in Tyrol: limited land resources, a huge debate on construction grounds, on tourism issues, on questions of spatial planning. If one does not let the communities develop their necessary compensatory effect and give them no authority, especially those communities which were expropriated in 1962; in the 1960s that was where that was transferred – then I must say, it is a bad sign for Tyrolean democracy if the federal legislation is not implemented there. And now one may even try to change, so to speak, a constitutional law around the back. That can't be it, it can't be! I am convinced that, as a result of the pressure of the Tyrolean population, there will have to be the corresponding changes. Thank you.
